Addressing Marine and Coastal Governance Conflicts at the Interface of Multiple Sectors and Jurisdictions Manuel Bellanger 1* , Cameron Speir 2 , Fabian Blanchard 3 , Kate Brooks 4 , James R. A. Butler 5 , Scott Crosson 6 , Robert Fonner 7 , Sophie Gourguet 1 , Daniel S. Holland 7 , Sakari Kuikka 8 , Bertrand Le Gallic 9 , Rebecca Lent 10 , Gary D. Libecap 11 , Douglas W. Lipton 12 , Prateep Kumar Nayak 13 , David Reid 14 , Pierre Scemama 1 , Robert Stephenson 15 , Olivier Thébaud 1 and Juliette C. Young 16,17 1 Ifremer, Univ Brest, CNRS, UMR 6308 AMURE, Unité d’Economie Maritime, IUEM, Plouzané, France, 2 Southwest Fisheries Science Center, NOAA Fisheries, Santa Cruz, CA, United States, 3 Ifremer, USR 3456 LEEISA (CNRS, Université de Guyane, Ifremer), Cayenne, France, 4 School of Humanities and Social Sciences, Deakin University, Melbourne, VIC, Australia, 5 CSIRO Land and Water, Brisbane, QLD, Australia, 6 Southeast Fisheries Science Center, NOAA Fisheries, Miami, FL, United States, 7 Northwest Fisheries Science Center, NOAA Fisheries, Seattle, WA, United States, 8 Ecosystems and Environment Research Program, University of Helsinki, Helsinki, Finland, 9 Univ Brest, Ifremer, CNRS, UMR 6308 AMURE, Brest, France, 10 International Whaling Commission, Cambridge, United Kingdom, 11 Bren School of Environmental Science, Edited by:         UC Santa Barbara, Santa Barbara, CA, United States, 12 NOAA Fisheries, Silver Spring, MD, United States, 13 School of Antonio Di Franco,        Environment, Enterprise and Development, University of Waterloo, Waterloo, ON, Canada, 14 Marine Institute, Galway, Stazione Zoologica Anton Dohrn, Italy       Ireland, 15 Fisheries and Oceans Canada, St. Andrews Biological Station, St. Andrews, NB, Canada, 16 UK Center of Ecology & Hydrology, Edinburgh, United Kingdom, 17 Agroécologie, AgroSup Dijon, Institut National de Recherche pour l’Agriculture, Reviewed by: l’Alimentation et l’Environnement (INRAE), University Bourgogne Franche-Comté, Dijon, France Robert Blasiak, Stockholm University, Sweden Elena Ojea, Marine and coastal activities are closely interrelated, and conflicts among different University of Vigo, Spain sectors can undermine management and conservation objectives. Governance systems *Correspondence: Manuel Bellanger        for fisheries, power generation, irrigation, aquaculture, marine biodiversity conservation, [EMAIL_REDACTED]      and other coastal and maritime activities are typically organized to manage conflicts within sectors, rather than across them. Based on the discussions around eight case Specialty section: This article was submitted to      studies presented at a workshop held in Brest in June 2019, this paper explores Marine Conservation       institutional approaches to move beyond managing conflicts within a sector. We primarily and Sustainability, a section of the journal focus on cases where the groups and sectors involved are heterogeneous in terms Frontiers in Marine Science       of: the jurisdiction they fall under; their objectives; and the way they value ecosystem Received: 27 March 2020           services. The paper first presents a synthesis of frameworks for understanding Accepted: 14 August 2020 and managing cross-sectoral governance conflicts, drawing from social and natural Published: 11 September 2020 sciences. We highlight commonalities but also conceptual differences across disciplines Citation: Bellanger M, Speir C, Blanchard F,      to address these issues. We then propose a novel analytical framework which we used Brooks K, Butler JRA, Crosson S,        to evaluate the eight case studies. Based on the main lessons learned from case studies, Fonner R, Gourguet S, Holland DS, Kuikka S, Le Gallic B, Lent R, we then discuss the feasibility and key determinants of stakeholder collaboration as Libecap GD, Lipton DW, Nayak PK,          well as compensation and incentive schemes. The discussion concludes with future Reid D, Scemama P, Stephenson R, research needs to support policy development and inform integrated institutional Thébaud O and Young JC (2020) Addressing Marine and Coastal         regimes that consider the diversity of stakeholder interests and the potential benefits Governance Conflicts at the Interface      of cross-sectoral coordination. of Multiple Sectors and Jurisdictions. (4) Management of Natural and         Measures based on EU Common Fisheries                 International science and policy               Individual Transferable Quotas (but Reared Salmon stocks in the           Policy, supported by national restrictions            collaboration within existing EU               not currently transferable between Baltic Sea                                                                                  institutions                                   river-based and at-sea fisheries) (5) Interactions between seals        Prohibition of killing or harming seals               Collective rules to mitigate impacts on and commercial fishing in Ireland                                                           both seals and fisheries (6) Water supply and salmon in        Endangered species laws and administrative            Collaborative governance program and           Water markets California’s Central Valley           biological opinions regulate water use                Council to facilitate stakeholder involvement (7) St. Croix River alewife           Top-down legislation (blocking fish passage)          Emerging collaborative partnership to restoration                           changed in response to pressure from many             overcome jurisdictional complexity. groups (8) Depredation by toothed            TAC reduction imposed on fishery depending            Partnership of diverse stakeholder             Incentive scheme: quota allocation whales in French Antarctic            on depredation level                                  representatives                                depends on fishing firm participation toothfish fishery                                                                                                                          to research on whale conservation and solutions to mitigate depredation [SOURCE: Page_14]

Barriers to other possible solutions                                             Opportunities [SOURCE: Page_14]

(1) Moray Firth Seal Management Plan       Lack of financial support from government to maintain local             Decline in salmon stocks creating renewed crisis and coordination and research; apathy from stakeholders as a result         elevating salmon conservation imperative (2) Salmon management institutions in      Conflicting legal mandates (under ESA, MMPA, and MSA), multiple Shift in norms and values may create impetus to amend the the Columbia River Basin                   jurisdictions (state, federal, tribal) and management agencies  ESA to allow consideration of trade-offs between objectives (3) International Whaling Commission       Conflicting national or treaty mandates, lack of global or regional     Collaboration, Memorandums of Understanding, joint agreements; no consensus on priority of       Increasing acceptance that return of alewife would improve objectives among interested parties and ecological health of the        productivity and not negatively impact the recreational waterway                                                                fishery (8) Depredation by toothed whales in       Management still focused on short-term issues, scientific               Longer-term management plans, test of new technical French Antarctic toothfish fishery         uncertainty about mitigation benefits and costs, importance of          mitigation solutions, further inclusion of fisheries impacts on non-use values                                                          marine mammals in MSC labeling criteria [SOURCE: Page_14]
